People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... I guess the best way to say it is I came in because nothing feels like mine anymore. It's just gone. And I mean everything. I’ve been a bartender for a long time, and I used to really take pride in it, you know? I’d get genuinely excited about making a good drink, getting the mix exactly right, the whole rhythm of a busy night behind the counter. But lately, I just stand there behind the rail. I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. The orders come in and I just feel completely blank. 

And it’s not just work, it’s my whole life outside of it, too. I used to be an active guy, always doing something. I’d go for these long runs to clear my head and get my blood pumping, or I'd get together and play basketball. I used to really love basketball, just the energy of it, being out there on the court. I even used to get excited about the street murals around the city, just walking around looking at the art, going out of my way to see a new one that went up. It sounds like small stuff, but those things were my life. They were me. And now... I could walk past a brand new mural, or someone could hand me a basketball, and I just don't care. I look at it all and there's just nothing there. I don't know how else to explain it, but it’s like all the things that used to make me who I am just don't matter to me at all anymore, and I just don't know what to do with myself when I feel like this...

Well, it's... I mean, I guess it's everything, really. It's not just the basketball or the murals or the bar. It's like... you know, I used to go over to my sister's place on Sundays. She makes this big roast, and the kids are running around, and it used to be the highlight of my week. I'd sit there and joke with my brother-in-law, maybe have a beer, just feel like I was part of something, you know? But the last few times I went, I just... I sat on the couch and I felt like I was watching them through a pane of glass. I could see them laughing, I could hear the kids yelling, but it didn't reach me. It was just noise. 

And my buddies, the guys I used to play ball with, they've been calling. "Hey, let's grab a drink," or "Hey, you want to come over and watch the game?" And I just let it go to voicemail. I can't even bring myself to pick up the phone. It's not that I'm mad at them or anything, it's just... the thought of sitting there, trying to make conversation, trying to pretend I care about who's winning or what's going on at their jobs... it just feels so exhausting. It's like I have to put on this heavy suit just to interact with people, and I just don't have the energy for it anymore. So I just stay home. I sit in my apartment and I just... I don't do anything. I don't even turn on the TV half the time. I just sit there and the hours go by, and it's like I'm just waiting for the day to be over, but then the next day is exactly the same. It's just this big, gray nothing covering everything I used to care about.

Yeah, that’s exactly it. It really is absolutely everything. Like I was trying to say when I first sat down, that’s the whole reason I came in, because nothing feels like mine anymore. It’s like someone came in and boxed up my entire personality and took it away in the middle of the night. 

I mean, I go back to the things I mentioned earlier—the murals, the runs, the basketball, even just the simple act of making a really good drink behind the bar. I used to get so fired up about all of that. I'd be out on a run, feeling the pavement under my feet, feeling my lungs work, and I'd feel alive, you know? Or I'd be walking down the block and spot a new piece of street art, all those colors just popping off the brick, and I’d actually get a rush from it. I'd stand there for ten minutes just taking it in. And making a drink—getting the ice right, the garnish, the exact pour—it was an art to me. I took real pride in it. 

But now? Now I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I hold a shaker at work, or I walk right past a mural that would have stopped me in my tracks a year ago, and there's just... nothing. Not a spark. Not a single drop of enjoyment. It’s exactly like you said, it’s absolutely everything. I just don't care anymore. I could win the lottery tomorrow and I swear to you I’d probably just shrug. It's just a total blank, and it's exhausting trying to remember what it even felt like to enjoy something.

I don't know how else to explain it, but it’s like all the things that used to make me who I am just don't matter to me at all anymore, and I just don't know what to do with myself when I feel like this...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a decrease in pleasure or interest in any other activities besides work and basketball, like hobbies or time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, I guess it's everything, really. It's not just the basketball or the murals or the bar. It's like... you know, I used to go over to my sister's place on Sundays. She makes this big roast, and the kids are running around, and it used to be the highlight of my week. I'd sit there and joke with my brother-in-law, maybe have a beer, just feel like I was part of something, you know? But the last few times I went, I just... I sat on the couch and I felt like I was watching them through a pane of glass. I could see them laughing, I could hear the kids yelling, but it didn't reach me. It was just noise. \n\nAnd my buddies, the guys I used to play ball with, they've been calling. \"Hey, let's grab a drink,\" or \"Hey, you want to come over and watch the game?\" And I just let it go to voicemail. I can't even bring myself to pick up the phone. It's not that I'm mad at them or anything, it's just... the thought of sitting there, trying to make conversation, trying to pretend I care about who's winning or what's going on at their jobs... it just feels so exhausting. It's like I have to put on this heavy suit just to interact with people, and I just don't have the energy for it anymore. So I just stay home. I sit in my apartment and I just... I don't do anything. I don't even turn on the TV half the time. I just sit there and the hours go by, and it's like I'm just waiting for the day to be over, but then the next day is exactly the same. It's just this big, gray nothing covering everything I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, to the point where nothing gives you any sense of enjoyment anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly it. It really is absolutely everything. Like I was trying to say when I first sat down, that’s the whole reason I came in, because nothing feels like mine anymore. It’s like someone came in and boxed up my entire personality and took it away in the middle of the night. \n\nI mean, I go back to the things I mentioned earlier—the murals, the runs, the basketball, even just the simple act of making a really good drink behind the bar. I used to get so fired up about all of that. I'd be out on a run, feeling the pavement under my feet, feeling my lungs work, and I'd feel alive, you know? Or I'd be walking down the block and spot a new piece of street art, all those colors just popping off the brick, and I’d actually get a rush from it. I'd stand there for ten minutes just taking it in. And making a drink—getting the ice right, the garnish, the exact pour—it was an art to me. I took real pride in it. \n\nBut now? Now I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I hold a shaker at work, or I walk right past a mural that would have stopped me in my tracks a year ago, and there's just... nothing. Not a spark. Not a single drop of enjoyment. It’s exactly like you said, it’s absolutely everything. I just don't care anymore. I could win the lottery tomorrow and I swear to you I’d probably just shrug. It's just a total blank, and it's exhausting trying to remember what it even felt like to enjoy something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Down or depressed... see, that’s the funny thing about it. When I hear the word "depressed," I think of, you know, somebody crying all the time. Somebody who's just visibly miserable and weeping and can't stop talking about how sad they are. But I don't feel sad. I really don't. I haven't shed a single tear through any of this. If I felt sad, at least that would be a feeling, you know? At least I'd know my engine was still running, even if it was in reverse. 

But it's not sadness. I don't feel "down" in that way. Like, I wake up in the morning—usually around 10 or 11 since I've always worked those late closing shifts at the bar—and I just lie there looking at the plaster on the ceiling. I'm not lying there thinking about how terrible my life is or feeling sorry for myself. I just feel completely empty. It's just a void. 

Like the other day, I was flipping through the channels and this movie came on, one of those real heavy dramas that usually gets a reaction out of me, maybe makes me reflect on things. And I just sat there staring at the screen. I didn't feel a lump in my throat, I didn't feel bummed out. I just felt like I was watching lights blink on a box. So if you're asking if I feel sad or down most of the day... no, I don't think I do. It's more like I just don't feel anything at all. The whole day just stretches out in front of me like this long, flat highway with absolutely no scenery, and I'm just coasting down it in neutral. I almost wish I did feel sad, because at least then I'd have something to point to, but it's just this constant, heavy blankness from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally go back to sleep.

It’s like someone came in and boxed up my entire personality and took it away in the middle of the night. \n\nI mean, I go back to the things I mentioned earlier—the murals, the runs, the basketball, even just the simple act of making a really good drink behind the bar. I used to get so fired up about all of that. I'd be out on a run, feeling the pavement under my feet, feeling my lungs work, and I'd feel alive, you know? Or I'd be walking down the block and spot a new piece of street art, all those colors just popping off the brick, and I’d actually get a rush from it. I'd stand there for ten minutes just taking it in. And making a drink—getting the ice right, the garnish, the exact pour—it was an art to me. I took real pride in it. \n\nBut now? Now I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I hold a shaker at work, or I walk right past a mural that would have stopped me in my tracks a year ago, and there's just... nothing. Not a spark. Not a single drop of enjoyment. It’s exactly like you said, it’s absolutely everything. I just don't care anymore. I could win the lottery tomorrow and I swear to you I’d probably just shrug. It's just a total blank, and it's exhausting trying to remember what it even felt like to enjoy something.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Down or depressed... see, that’s the funny thing about it. When I hear the word \"depressed,\" I think of, you know, somebody crying all the time. Somebody who's just visibly miserable and weeping and can't stop talking about how sad they are. But I don't feel sad. I really don't. I haven't shed a single tear through any of this. If I felt sad, at least that would be a feeling, you know? At least I'd know my engine was still running, even if it was in reverse. \n\nBut it's not sadness. I don't feel \"down\" in that way. Like, I wake up in the morning—usually around 10 or 11 since I've always worked those late closing shifts at the bar—and I just lie there looking at the plaster on the ceiling. I'm not lying there thinking about how terrible my life is or feeling sorry for myself. I just feel completely empty. It's just a void. \n\nLike the other day, I was flipping through the channels and this movie came on, one of those real heavy dramas that usually gets a reaction out of me, maybe makes me reflect on things. And I just sat there staring at the screen. I didn't feel a lump in my throat, I didn't feel bummed out. I just felt like I was watching lights blink on a box. So if you're asking if I feel sad or down most of the day... no, I don't think I do. It's more like I just don't feel anything at all. The whole day just stretches out in front of me like this long, flat highway with absolutely no scenery, and I'm just coasting down it in neutral. I almost wish I did feel sad, because at least then I'd have something to point to, but it's just this constant, heavy blankness from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally go back to sleep.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt fatigued or like you have no energy most days, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / bartender / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fatigue\nmanifestation: \"By the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I've already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Energy? Yeah... I mean, I don't even know if "fatigue" is a big enough word for it. It's more like my bones are made of lead. 

I've been behind the bar for years, right? You get used to being on your feet. You get used to the hustle—the moving around, shaking, pouring, hauling buckets of ice from the back. It's a physical job, but I always had the stamina for it. I could work a Friday night rush, close down at 3 AM, scrub the mats, and still have energy to spare. I never used to think twice about it. 

But lately... man, it's just ridiculous. I'll wake up, like I said, around 10 or 11, and I haven't even done anything. I've just been lying there. But getting out of bed feels like I'm trying to walk underwater. Getting dressed, making coffee... it all just takes so much effort. And then I drag myself into work for my shift, and I swear, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor from the stockroom, or just stand there and set up the well for the night—getting the speed rack sorted, cutting the limes, stocking the napkins—my body feels like I've already worked a double. 

And this is before the doors even open! I haven't even served a single customer yet, I've barely did anything that day, and I'm just completely wiped out. It's not a normal tired. It's this deep, heavy exhaustion that just sits in my muscles. I'll just stand there leaning against the back bar, staring at the bottles, wondering how I'm going to make it through the next eight hours when lifting a single bottle of vodka feels like lifting a cinderblock. It doesn't matter how much I sleep, either. I could lie in bed for twelve hours and I still show up feeling like I just ran a marathon in combat boots. It's just... it's every single day. I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank.

guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I let down the younger bartenders and the artists I promised space to, and it feels like I deserve whatever happens to me for wrecking their shot.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: Inventory used to be automatic for me, but now I stare at the bottles and lose count three times, or I freeze trying to decide something simple like what garnish to restock.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: Before I sank into this after the new owners took over, I never believed I had ruined the place or that people were being punished because of me. Those thoughts only started once I got depressed these past couple months.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I've missed several shifts and made closing mistakes I never used to make, and now my coworkers have to double-check work that people used to trust me with. I also stopped seeing friends for runs, basketball, or mural shoots because I can't make myself show up.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, since the bar was bought out and everything I had been working toward got changed.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / bartender / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, to the point where nothing gives you any sense of enjoyment anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly it. It really is absolutely everything. Like I was trying to say when I first sat down, that’s the whole reason I came in, because nothing feels like mine anymore. It’s like someone came in and boxed up my entire personality and took it away in the middle of the night. \n\nI mean, I go back to the things I mentioned earlier—the murals, the runs, the basketball, even just the simple act of making a really good drink behind the bar. I used to get so fired up about all of that. I'd be out on a run, feeling the pavement under my feet, feeling my lungs work, and I'd feel alive, you know? Or I'd be walking down the block and spot a new piece of street art, all those colors just popping off the brick, and I’d actually get a rush from it. I'd stand there for ten minutes just taking it in. And making a drink—getting the ice right, the garnish, the exact pour—it was an art to me. I took real pride in it. \n\nBut now? Now I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I hold a shaker at work, or I walk right past a mural that would have stopped me in my tracks a year ago, and there's just... nothing. Not a spark. Not a single drop of enjoyment. It’s exactly like you said, it’s absolutely everything. I just don't care anymore. I could win the lottery tomorrow and I swear to you I’d probably just shrug. It's just a total blank, and it's exhausting trying to remember what it even felt like to enjoy something.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Down or depressed... see, that’s the funny thing about it. When I hear the word \"depressed,\" I think of, you know, somebody crying all the time. Somebody who's just visibly miserable and weeping and can't stop talking about how sad they are. But I don't feel sad. I really don't. I haven't shed a single tear through any of this. If I felt sad, at least that would be a feeling, you know? At least I'd know my engine was still running, even if it was in reverse. \n\nBut it's not sadness. I don't feel \"down\" in that way. Like, I wake up in the morning—usually around 10 or 11 since I've always worked those late closing shifts at the bar—and I just lie there looking at the plaster on the ceiling. I'm not lying there thinking about how terrible my life is or feeling sorry for myself. I just feel completely empty. It's just a void. \n\nLike the other day, I was flipping through the channels and this movie came on, one of those real heavy dramas that usually gets a reaction out of me, maybe makes me reflect on things. And I just sat there staring at the screen. I didn't feel a lump in my throat, I didn't feel bummed out. I just felt like I was watching lights blink on a box. So if you're asking if I feel sad or down most of the day... no, I don't think I do. It's more like I just don't feel anything at all. The whole day just stretches out in front of me like this long, flat highway with absolutely no scenery, and I'm just coasting down it in neutral. I almost wish I did feel sad, because at least then I'd have something to point to, but it's just this constant, heavy blankness from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally go back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt fatigued or like you have no energy most days, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\nPatient: Energy? Yeah... I mean, I don't even know if \"fatigue\" is a big enough word for it. It's more like my bones are made of lead. \n\nI've been behind the bar for years, right? You get used to being on your feet. You get used to the hustle—the moving around, shaking, pouring, hauling buckets of ice from the back. It's a physical job, but I always had the stamina for it. I could work a Friday night rush, close down at 3 AM, scrub the mats, and still have energy to spare. I never used to think twice about it. \n\nBut lately... man, it's just ridiculous. I'll wake up, like I said, around 10 or 11, and I haven't even done anything. I've just been lying there. But getting out of bed feels like I'm trying to walk underwater. Getting dressed, making coffee... it all just takes so much effort. And then I drag myself into work for my shift, and I swear, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor from the stockroom, or just stand there and set up the well for the night—getting the speed rack sorted, cutting the limes, stocking the napkins—my body feels like I've already worked a double. \n\nAnd this is before the doors even open! I haven't even served a single customer yet, I've barely did anything that day, and I'm just completely wiped out. It's not a normal tired. It's this deep, heavy exhaustion that just sits in my muscles. I'll just stand there leaning against the back bar, staring at the bottles, wondering how I'm going to make it through the next eight hours when lifting a single bottle of vodka feels like lifting a cinderblock. It doesn't matter how much I sleep, either. I could lie in bed for twelve hours and I still show up feeling like I just ran a marathon in combat boots. It's just... it's every single day. I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Weight? Appetite? Yeah, I mean... it's funny you ask that, because food just doesn't even cross my mind anymore. It's like my brain just deleted the whole concept of eating. 

I used to be the guy who would plan out his meals, you know? Even working those late shifts, I'd always make sure I had a good, solid dinner before heading in. Maybe I'd hit up that little Italian place down the street from my apartment, grab a chicken parm sub, or I'd make something decent at home. And then after a shift, when you're all keyed up from the rush and the noise, me and a couple of the other guys from the bar would usually go grab late-night tacos or a slice of pizza to wind down. It was part of the routine. It was something to look forward to.

But now? Man, I just... I don't even think about it. Most days, I completely forget to eat. I'll wake up, like I said, late, just lie there feeling that heavy emptiness, drag myself to work, and I won't even realize I haven't eaten anything all day until I'm standing behind the bar, maybe four or five hours into my shift, and my hands start shaking. I'll be trying to pour a draft or measure out a shot, and my hands are just trembling because my blood sugar has completely tanked. And even then, I don't feel hungry. I just feel weak. 

So I'll try to force myself to eat something later. Usually, it's not until after we close, after we've locked the doors and I'm doing the final wipe-down of the counters. I might go into the back and grab whatever's left in the employee fridge—a cold piece of pizza from someone else's lunch, half a sandwich, whatever. I'll take a few bites of it, just standing there under those harsh fluorescent lights, and honestly... I just feel sick looking at it. It tastes like cardboard. It feels like ash in my mouth. I chew it and chew it, and I just can't bring myself to swallow it half the time. My stomach just sort of clenches up, like it's rejecting the very idea of food. I've definitely dropped a few belt notches, I can tell you that much, because my work pants are practically falling off me these days. But the thought of sitting down and actually eating a full meal? I just can't do it. It's just another thing that takes energy I don't have.
=====================================

It was just noise. \n\nAnd my buddies, the guys I used to play ball with, they've been calling. \"Hey, let's grab a drink,\" or \"Hey, you want to come over and watch the game?\" And I just let it go to voicemail. I can't even bring myself to pick up the phone. It's not that I'm mad at them or anything, it's just... the thought of sitting there, trying to make conversation, trying to pretend I care about who's winning or what's going on at their jobs... it just feels so exhausting. It's like I have to put on this heavy suit just to interact with people, and I just don't have the energy for it anymore. So I just stay home. I sit in my apartment and I just... I don't do anything. I don't even turn on the TV half the time. I just sit there and the hours go by, and it's like I'm just waiting for the day to be over, but then the next day is exactly the same. It's just this big, gray nothing covering everything I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, to the point where nothing gives you any sense of enjoyment anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly it. It really is absolutely everything. Like I was trying to say when I first sat down, that’s the whole reason I came in, because nothing feels like mine anymore. I used to get so fired up about all of that. I'd be out on a run, feeling the pavement under my feet, feeling my lungs work, and I'd feel alive, you know? Or I'd be walking down the block and spot a new piece of street art, all those colors just popping off the brick, and I’d actually get a rush from it. I'd stand there for ten minutes just taking it in. And making a drink—getting the ice right, the garnish, the exact pour—it was an art to me. I took real pride in it. \n\nBut now? Now I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I hold a shaker at work, or I walk right past a mural that would have stopped me in my tracks a year ago, and there's just... nothing. Not a spark. Not a single drop of enjoyment. It’s exactly like you said, it’s absolutely everything. I just don't care anymore. I could win the lottery tomorrow and I swear to you I’d probably just shrug. It's just a total blank, and it's exhausting trying to remember what it even felt like to enjoy something.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Down or depressed... see, that’s the funny thing about it. When I hear the word \"depressed,\" I think of, you know, somebody crying all the time. Somebody who's just visibly miserable and weeping and can't stop talking about how sad they are. But I don't feel sad. I really don't. I haven't shed a single tear through any of this. If I felt sad, at least that would be a feeling, you know? At least I'd know my engine was still running, even if it was in reverse. \n\nBut it's not sadness. I don't feel \"down\" in that way. Like, I wake up in the morning—usually around 10 or 11 since I've always worked those late closing shifts at the bar—and I just lie there looking at the plaster on the ceiling. I'm not lying there thinking about how terrible my life is or feeling sorry for myself. I just feel completely empty. It's just a void. \n\nLike the other day, I was flipping through the channels and this movie came on, one of those real heavy dramas that usually gets a reaction out of me, maybe makes me reflect on things. And I just sat there staring at the screen. I didn't feel a lump in my throat, I didn't feel bummed out. I just felt like I was watching lights blink on a box. So if you're asking if I feel sad or down most of the day... no, I don't think I do. It's more like I just don't feel anything at all. The whole day just stretches out in front of me like this long, flat highway with absolutely no scenery, and I'm just coasting down it in neutral. I almost wish I did feel sad, because at least then I'd have something to point to, but it's just this constant, heavy blankness from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally go back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt fatigued or like you have no energy most days, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\nPatient: Energy? Yeah... I mean, I don't even know if \"fatigue\" is a big enough word for it. It's more like my bones are made of lead. \n\nI've been behind the bar for years, right? You get used to being on your feet. You get used to the hustle—the moving around, shaking, pouring, hauling buckets of ice from the back. It's a physical job, but I always had the stamina for it. I could work a Friday night rush, close down at 3 AM, scrub the mats, and still have energy to spare. I never used to think twice about it. \n\nBut lately... man, it's just ridiculous. I'll wake up, like I said, around 10 or 11, and I haven't even done anything. I've just been lying there. But getting out of bed feels like I'm trying to walk underwater. Getting dressed, making coffee... it all just takes so much effort. And then I drag myself into work for my shift, and I swear, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor from the stockroom, or just stand there and set up the well for the night—getting the speed rack sorted, cutting the limes, stocking the napkins—my body feels like I've already worked a double. \n\nAnd this is before the doors even open! I haven't even served a single customer yet, I've barely did anything that day, and I'm just completely wiped out. It's not a normal tired. It's this deep, heavy exhaustion that just sits in my muscles. I'll just stand there leaning against the back bar, staring at the bottles, wondering how I'm going to make it through the next eight hours when lifting a single bottle of vodka feels like lifting a cinderblock. It doesn't matter how much I sleep, either. I could lie in bed for twelve hours and I still show up feeling like I just ran a marathon in combat boots. It's just... it's every single day. I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: Weight? Appetite? Yeah, I mean... it's funny you ask that, because food just doesn't even cross my mind anymore. It's like my brain just deleted the whole concept of eating. \n\nI used to be the guy who would plan out his meals, you know? Even working those late shifts, I'd always make sure I had a good, solid dinner before heading in. Maybe I'd hit up that little Italian place down the street from my apartment, grab a chicken parm sub, or I'd make something decent at home. And then after a shift, when you're all keyed up from the rush and the noise, me and a couple of the other guys from the bar would usually go grab late-night tacos or a slice of pizza to wind down. It was part of the routine. It was something to look forward to.\n\nBut now? Man, I just... I don't even think about it. Most days, I completely forget to eat. I'll wake up, like I said, late, just lie there feeling that heavy emptiness, drag myself to work, and I won't even realize I haven't eaten anything all day until I'm standing behind the bar, maybe four or five hours into my shift, and my hands start shaking. I'll be trying to pour a draft or measure out a shot, and my hands are just trembling because my blood sugar has completely tanked. And even then, I don't feel hungry. I just feel weak. \n\nSo I'll try to force myself to eat something later. Usually, it's not until after we close, after we've locked the doors and I'm doing the final wipe-down of the counters. I might go into the back and grab whatever's left in the employee fridge—a cold piece of pizza from someone else's lunch, half a sandwich, whatever. I'll take a few bites of it, just standing there under those harsh fluorescent lights, and honestly... I just feel sick looking at it. It tastes like cardboard. It feels like ash in my mouth. I chew it and chew it, and I just can't bring myself to swallow it half the time. My stomach just sort of clenches up, like it's rejecting the very idea of food. I've definitely dropped a few belt notches, I can tell you that much, because my work pants are practically falling off me these days. But the thought of sitting down and actually eating a full meal? I just can't do it. It's just another thing that takes energy I don't have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I'll be trying to pour a draft or measure out a shot, and my hands are just trembling because my blood sugar has completely tanked. And even then, I don't feel hungry. I just feel weak. \n\nSo I'll try to force myself to eat something later. Usually, it's not until after we close, after we've locked the doors and I'm doing the final wipe-down of the counters. I might go into the back and grab whatever's left in the employee fridge—a cold piece of pizza from someone else's lunch, half a sandwich, whatever. I'll take a few bites of it, just standing there under those harsh fluorescent lights, and honestly... I just feel sick looking at it. It tastes like cardboard. It feels like ash in my mouth. I chew it and chew it, and I just can't bring myself to swallow it half the time. My stomach just sort of clenches up, like it's rejecting the very idea of food. I've definitely dropped a few belt notches, I can tell you that much, because my work pants are practically falling off me these days. But the thought of sitting down and actually eating a full meal? I just can't do it. It's just another thing that takes energy I don't have.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things you've done—or haven't done—recently?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / bartender / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep thinking I let down the younger bartenders and the artists I promised space to, and it feels like I deserve whatever happens to me for wrecking their shot.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty... yeah. That’s pretty much the only thing I *do* feel these days, now that you mention it. Just this... this constant, gnawing guilt. It’s like, I look around the bar, and I just see all these people I’m completely failing. 

See, I’m the senior guy there. I’ve been slinging drinks longer than some of our barbacks have been alive. And because of that, people look up to me, or at least they used to. I’ve got these younger bartenders—kids, really, early twenties, trying to pay off student loans or whatever—and I promised them I’d show them the ropes. I told them I’d train them up, teach them how to handle the Friday night rush, how to manage a crowd without losing your mind, how to read a room, so they could start getting those prime shifts where the real tip money is. But now? I can barely keep my own eyes open, let alone mentor anybody. When we get slammed, I’m moving so slow, I’m just dead weight, and I see them scrambling, sweating, trying to cover my station on top of theirs. I’m completely letting them down. I promised I’d look out for them, and instead, I’m just dragging them under with me. 

And it’s not just the staff, either. A while back, I talked the owner into letting me use that big exposed brick wall near the pool tables to showcase local artists. Give them a month to hang their stuff, maybe sell a few pieces to the regulars, you know? Get their name out there, give them a little spotlight. I had this whole list of folks I promised space to. There was this one kid who does these amazing street photography prints... I looked him right in the eye and told him, "Next month, that wall is yours." But I never made the calls. I never organized it. The wall's just sitting there empty because I can't even muster the energy to send a simple text message. 

I just keep thinking about how I wrecked their shot. All of them. The younger staff, the artists... they were counting on me to come through, and I just blew it. And honestly? When I'm standing there at 3 AM, wiping down the same spot on the mahogany for the fifth time, I just think about how I deserve whatever happens to me. I deserve this... this whole miserable, empty situation. I ruined their chances because I'm just too broken to do my job, and it feels like this heavy blankness I've been telling you about is just my punishment for it. I mean, what kind of guy makes promises like that to people who are just trying to get a leg up, and then just checks out? A worthless one, that's who. I just... I can't stop thinking about how much I failed them. It just plays on a loop in my head all day.
=====================================

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.8, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I guess the best way to say it is I came in because nothing feels like mine anymore. It's just gone. And I mean everything. I’ve been a bartender for a long time, and I used to really take pride in it, you know? I’d get genuinely excited about making a good drink, getting the mix exactly right, the whole rhythm of a busy night behind the counter. But lately, I just stand there behind the rail. I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. The orders come in and I just feel completely blank. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, it’s my whole life outside of it, too. I used to be an active guy, always doing something. I’d go for these long runs to clear my head and get my blood pumping, or I'd get together and play basketball. I used to really love basketball, just the energy of it, being out there on the court. I even used to get excited about the street murals around the city, just walking around looking at the art, going out of my way to see a new one that went up. It sounds like small stuff, but those things were my life. They were me. And now... I could walk past a brand new mural, or someone could hand me a basketball, and I just don't care. I look at it all and there's just nothing there. I don't know how else to explain it, but it’s like all the things that used to make me who I am just don't matter to me at all anymore, and I just don't know what to do with myself when I feel like this...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a decrease in pleasure or interest in any other activities besides work and basketball, like hobbies or time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, I guess it's everything, really. It's not just the basketball or the murals or the bar. It's like... you know, I used to go over to my sister's place on Sundays. She makes this big roast, and the kids are running around, and it used to be the highlight of my week. I'd sit there and joke with my brother-in-law, maybe have a beer, just feel like I was part of something, you know? But the last few times I went, I just... I sat on the couch and I felt like I was watching them through a pane of glass. I could see them laughing, I could hear the kids yelling, but it didn't reach me. It was just noise. \n\nAnd my buddies, the guys I used to play ball with, they've been calling. \"Hey, let's grab a drink,\" or \"Hey, you want to come over and watch the game?\" And I just let it go to voicemail. I can't even bring myself to pick up the phone. It's not that I'm mad at them or anything, it's just... the thought of sitting there, trying to make conversation, trying to pretend I care about who's winning or what's going on at their jobs... it just feels so exhausting. It's like I have to put on this heavy suit just to interact with people, and I just don't have the energy for it anymore. So I just stay home. I sit in my apartment and I just... I don't do anything. I don't even turn on the TV half the time. I just sit there and the hours go by, and it's like I'm just waiting for the day to be over, but then the next day is exactly the same. It's just this big, gray nothing covering everything I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, to the point where nothing gives you any sense of enjoyment anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly it. It really is absolutely everything. Like I was trying to say when I first sat down, that’s the whole reason I came in, because nothing feels like mine anymore. It’s like someone came in and boxed up my entire personality and took it away in the middle of the night. \n\nI mean, I go back to the things I mentioned earlier—the murals, the runs, the basketball, even just the simple act of making a really good drink behind the bar. I used to get so fired up about all of that. I'd be out on a run, feeling the pavement under my feet, feeling my lungs work, and I'd feel alive, you know? Or I'd be walking down the block and spot a new piece of street art, all those colors just popping off the brick, and I’d actually get a rush from it. I'd stand there for ten minutes just taking it in. And making a drink—getting the ice right, the garnish, the exact pour—it was an art to me. I took real pride in it. \n\nBut now? Now I just stand there like I don't care about any of it. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I hold a shaker at work, or I walk right past a mural that would have stopped me in my tracks a year ago, and there's just... nothing. Not a spark. Not a single drop of enjoyment. It’s exactly like you said, it’s absolutely everything. I just don't care anymore. I could win the lottery tomorrow and I swear to you I’d probably just shrug. It's just a total blank, and it's exhausting trying to remember what it even felt like to enjoy something.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Down or depressed... see, that’s the funny thing about it. When I hear the word \"depressed,\" I think of, you know, somebody crying all the time. Somebody who's just visibly miserable and weeping and can't stop talking about how sad they are. But I don't feel sad. I really don't. I haven't shed a single tear through any of this. If I felt sad, at least that would be a feeling, you know? At least I'd know my engine was still running, even if it was in reverse. \n\nBut it's not sadness. I don't feel \"down\" in that way. Like, I wake up in the morning—usually around 10 or 11 since I've always worked those late closing shifts at the bar—and I just lie there looking at the plaster on the ceiling. I'm not lying there thinking about how terrible my life is or feeling sorry for myself. I just feel completely empty. It's just a void. \n\nLike the other day, I was flipping through the channels and this movie came on, one of those real heavy dramas that usually gets a reaction out of me, maybe makes me reflect on things. And I just sat there staring at the screen. I didn't feel a lump in my throat, I didn't feel bummed out. I just felt like I was watching lights blink on a box. So if you're asking if I feel sad or down most of the day... no, I don't think I do. It's more like I just don't feel anything at all. The whole day just stretches out in front of me like this long, flat highway with absolutely no scenery, and I'm just coasting down it in neutral. I almost wish I did feel sad, because at least then I'd have something to point to, but it's just this constant, heavy blankness from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally go back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt fatigued or like you have no energy most days, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\nPatient: Energy? Yeah... I mean, I don't even know if \"fatigue\" is a big enough word for it. It's more like my bones are made of lead. \n\nI've been behind the bar for years, right? You get used to being on your feet. You get used to the hustle—the moving around, shaking, pouring, hauling buckets of ice from the back. It's a physical job, but I always had the stamina for it. I could work a Friday night rush, close down at 3 AM, scrub the mats, and still have energy to spare. I never used to think twice about it. \n\nBut lately... man, it's just ridiculous. I'll wake up, like I said, around 10 or 11, and I haven't even done anything. I've just been lying there. But getting out of bed feels like I'm trying to walk underwater. Getting dressed, making coffee... it all just takes so much effort. And then I drag myself into work for my shift, and I swear, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor from the stockroom, or just stand there and set up the well for the night—getting the speed rack sorted, cutting the limes, stocking the napkins—my body feels like I've already worked a double. \n\nAnd this is before the doors even open! I haven't even served a single customer yet, I've barely did anything that day, and I'm just completely wiped out. It's not a normal tired. It's this deep, heavy exhaustion that just sits in my muscles. I'll just stand there leaning against the back bar, staring at the bottles, wondering how I'm going to make it through the next eight hours when lifting a single bottle of vodka feels like lifting a cinderblock. It doesn't matter how much I sleep, either. I could lie in bed for twelve hours and I still show up feeling like I just ran a marathon in combat boots. It's just... it's every single day. I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: Weight? Appetite? Yeah, I mean... it's funny you ask that, because food just doesn't even cross my mind anymore. It's like my brain just deleted the whole concept of eating. \n\nI used to be the guy who would plan out his meals, you know? Even working those late shifts, I'd always make sure I had a good, solid dinner before heading in. Maybe I'd hit up that little Italian place down the street from my apartment, grab a chicken parm sub, or I'd make something decent at home. And then after a shift, when you're all keyed up from the rush and the noise, me and a couple of the other guys from the bar would usually go grab late-night tacos or a slice of pizza to wind down. It was part of the routine. It was something to look forward to.\n\nBut now? Man, I just... I don't even think about it. Most days, I completely forget to eat. I'll wake up, like I said, late, just lie there feeling that heavy emptiness, drag myself to work, and I won't even realize I haven't eaten anything all day until I'm standing behind the bar, maybe four or five hours into my shift, and my hands start shaking. I'll be trying to pour a draft or measure out a shot, and my hands are just trembling because my blood sugar has completely tanked. And even then, I don't feel hungry. I just feel weak. \n\nSo I'll try to force myself to eat something later. Usually, it's not until after we close, after we've locked the doors and I'm doing the final wipe-down of the counters. I might go into the back and grab whatever's left in the employee fridge—a cold piece of pizza from someone else's lunch, half a sandwich, whatever. I'll take a few bites of it, just standing there under those harsh fluorescent lights, and honestly... I just feel sick looking at it. It tastes like cardboard. It feels like ash in my mouth. I chew it and chew it, and I just can't bring myself to swallow it half the time. My stomach just sort of clenches up, like it's rejecting the very idea of food. I've definitely dropped a few belt notches, I can tell you that much, because my work pants are practically falling off me these days. But the thought of sitting down and actually eating a full meal? I just can't do it. It's just another thing that takes energy I don't have.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things you've done—or haven't done—recently?\n\nPatient: Guilty... yeah. That’s pretty much the only thing I *do* feel these days, now that you mention it. Just this... this constant, gnawing guilt. It’s like, I look around the bar, and I just see all these people I’m completely failing. \n\nSee, I’m the senior guy there. I’ve been slinging drinks longer than some of our barbacks have been alive. And because of that, people look up to me, or at least they used to. I’ve got these younger bartenders—kids, really, early twenties, trying to pay off student loans or whatever—and I promised them I’d show them the ropes. I told them I’d train them up, teach them how to handle the Friday night rush, how to manage a crowd without losing your mind, how to read a room, so they could start getting those prime shifts where the real tip money is. But now? I can barely keep my own eyes open, let alone mentor anybody. When we get slammed, I’m moving so slow, I’m just dead weight, and I see them scrambling, sweating, trying to cover my station on top of theirs. I’m completely letting them down. I promised I’d look out for them, and instead, I’m just dragging them under with me. \n\nAnd it’s not just the staff, either. A while back, I talked the owner into letting me use that big exposed brick wall near the pool tables to showcase local artists. Give them a month to hang their stuff, maybe sell a few pieces to the regulars, you know? Get their name out there, give them a little spotlight. I had this whole list of folks I promised space to. There was this one kid who does these amazing street photography prints... I looked him right in the eye and told him, \"Next month, that wall is yours.\" But I never made the calls. I never organized it. The wall's just sitting there empty because I can't even muster the energy to send a simple text message. \n\nI just keep thinking about how I wrecked their shot. All of them. The younger staff, the artists... they were counting on me to come through, and I just blew it. And honestly? When I'm standing there at 3 AM, wiping down the same spot on the mahogany for the fifth time, I just think about how I deserve whatever happens to me. I deserve this... this whole miserable, empty situation. I ruined their chances because I'm just too broken to do my job, and it feels like this heavy blankness I've been telling you about is just my punishment for it. I mean, what kind of guy makes promises like that to people who are just trying to get a leg up, and then just checks out? A worthless one, that's who. I just... I can't stop thinking about how much I failed them. It just plays on a loop in my head all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I'll just stand there leaning against the back bar, staring at the bottles, wondering how I'm going to make it through the next eight hours when lifting a single bottle of vodka feels like lifting a cinderblock. It doesn't matter how much I sleep, either. I could lie in bed for twelve hours and I still show up feeling like I just ran a marathon in combat boots. It's just... it's every single day. I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: Weight? Appetite? Yeah, I mean... it's funny you ask that, because food just doesn't even cross my mind anymore. It's like my brain just deleted the whole concept of eating. \n\nI used to be the guy who would plan out his meals, you know? Even working those late shifts, I'd always make sure I had a good, solid dinner before heading in. Maybe I'd hit up that little Italian place down the street from my apartment, grab a chicken parm sub, or I'd make something decent at home. And then after a shift, when you're all keyed up from the rush and the noise, me and a couple of the other guys from the bar would usually go grab late-night tacos or a slice of pizza to wind down. It was part of the routine. It was something to look forward to.\n\nBut now? Man, I just... I don't even think about it. Most days, I completely forget to eat. I'll wake up, like I said, late, just lie there feeling that heavy emptiness, drag myself to work, and I won't even realize I haven't eaten anything all day until I'm standing behind the bar, maybe four or five hours into my shift, and my hands start shaking. I'll be trying to pour a draft or measure out a shot, and my hands are just trembling because my blood sugar has completely tanked. And even then, I don't feel hungry. I just feel weak. \n\nSo I'll try to force myself to eat something later. Usually, it's not until after we close, after we've locked the doors and I'm doing the final wipe-down of the counters. I might go into the back and grab whatever's left in the employee fridge—a cold piece of pizza from someone else's lunch, half a sandwich, whatever. I'll take a few bites of it, just standing there under those harsh fluorescent lights, and honestly... I just feel sick looking at it. It tastes like cardboard. It feels like ash in my mouth. I chew it and chew it, and I just can't bring myself to swallow it half the time. My stomach just sort of clenches up, like it's rejecting the very idea of food. I've definitely dropped a few belt notches, I can tell you that much, because my work pants are practically falling off me these days. But the thought of sitting down and actually eating a full meal? I just can't do it. It's just another thing that takes energy I don't have.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things you've done—or haven't done—recently?\n\nPatient: Guilty... yeah. That’s pretty much the only thing I *do* feel these days, now that you mention it. Just this... this constant, gnawing guilt. It’s like, I look around the bar, and I just see all these people I’m completely failing. \n\nSee, I’m the senior guy there. I’ve been slinging drinks longer than some of our barbacks have been alive. And because of that, people look up to me, or at least they used to. I’ve got these younger bartenders—kids, really, early twenties, trying to pay off student loans or whatever—and I promised them I’d show them the ropes. I told them I’d train them up, teach them how to handle the Friday night rush, how to manage a crowd without losing your mind, how to read a room, so they could start getting those prime shifts where the real tip money is. But now? I can barely keep my own eyes open, let alone mentor anybody. When we get slammed, I’m moving so slow, I’m just dead weight, and I see them scrambling, sweating, trying to cover my station on top of theirs. I’m completely letting them down. I promised I’d look out for them, and instead, I’m just dragging them under with me. \n\nAnd it’s not just the staff, either. A while back, I talked the owner into letting me use that big exposed brick wall near the pool tables to showcase local artists. Give them a month to hang their stuff, maybe sell a few pieces to the regulars, you know? Get their name out there, give them a little spotlight. I had this whole list of folks I promised space to. There was this one kid who does these amazing street photography prints... I looked him right in the eye and told him, \"Next month, that wall is yours.\" But I never made the calls. I never organized it. The wall's just sitting there empty because I can't even muster the energy to send a simple text message. \n\nI just keep thinking about how I wrecked their shot. All of them. The younger staff, the artists... they were counting on me to come through, and I just blew it. And honestly? When I'm standing there at 3 AM, wiping down the same spot on the mahogany for the fifth time, I just think about how I deserve whatever happens to me. I deserve this... this whole miserable, empty situation. I ruined their chances because I'm just too broken to do my job, and it feels like this heavy blankness I've been telling you about is just my punishment for it. I mean, what kind of guy makes promises like that to people who are just trying to get a leg up, and then just checks out? A worthless one, that's who. I just... Dying? No... no, I haven't... it's not like that. I mean, don't get me wrong, doc, I feel like a walking corpse most days, with the way my body just drags and how heavy everything feels, but... actually wanting to be dead? No. It's more like... I just want to pause everything. I want the world to just stop spinning for a minute so I can catch my breath, you know? 

Working in a bar for as long as I have, you see a lot of guys who really do have a death wish. You see them come in, night after night, sitting at the exact same corner stool, knocking back cheap whiskey until they can't even see straight, just completely giving up on everything. I had this one regular a few years back who used to talk like that, always talking about how he was just done with it all, and... well, it didn't end well for him. I remember thinking back then, even when things were tough for me, that I never wanted to cross that line. 

So, no, I don't sit around thinking about ending it all or anything like that. I'm not looking for an exit. I just... I'm just so damn tired of feeling this way, of letting everyone down, of being this useless dead weight behind the counter. But I'm still here. I'm still clocking in, even if I'm barely functioning. I don't want to die, I just... I don't know how to keep living like this, if that makes any sense. It's just this constant, endless grind where I feel like I'm failing everyone, but I'm definitely not looking to check out for good. I just wish the guilt and the exhaustion would let up for just one single day.
